===== PAGE 1 =====
ASYMPTOTIC STABILITY OF THE DEGREE-ONE VORTEX IN THE
ABELIAN YANG-MILLS-HIGGS MODEL
JONAS L ¨UHRMANN, JOS ´E M. PALACIOS, FABIO PUSATERI, WILHELM SCHLAG,
AND SOHRAB SHAHSHAHANI
Abstract.We prove asymptotic stability of the degree-one vortex in thep1`2q-dimensional
abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs model at the self-dual coupling, for small equivariant perturbations
in weighted Sobolev spaces. The abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs model is a classical relativistic field
theory onp1`2q-dimensional Minkowski space, describing a complex-valued field coupled to an
electromagnetic potential and admitting topological solitons known as vortices.
This paper is the final and main part of a three-paper series. Under the orthogonal gauge
condition used here, perturbations of the vortex are governed by a system of nonlinear Klein-
Gordon equations for the dynamical variables, coupled to an elliptic equation for the temporal
component of the electromagnetic potential. The linearized operator has continuous spectrum
r1,8qand a single positive gap eigenvalue (internal mode) of multiplicity two, whose spectral
properties, associated distorted Fourier theory, and linear decay estimates are developed in the
two companion papers.
The main difficulty is the long-time analysis of the coupled radiation–internal-mode dynam-
ics. In two space dimensions the dispersive decay of the Klein-Gordon radiation is relatively
weak, while the internal mode decays only on the long time scale dictated by nonlinear radiation
damping. At the same time, the Klein-Gordon equations for the radiation contain non-spatially
localized variable coefficient quadratic interactions, which cannot be treated perturbatively and
require a normal form analysis. We prove decay of the radiation by combining a good-bad de-
composition, a flat-sharp decomposition, and a space-time resonance analysis carried out relative
to the flat Klein-Gordon flow. The passage between the flat analysis and the Klein-Gordon flow
with potential is achieved through integrated local energy decay and transference estimates de-
rived from the distorted Fourier theory. We expect this part of the nonlinear analysis to be of
independent interest.

1.Introduction
This paper is the third and final part of a three-part series, cf. [L¨ uh+26a; L¨ uh+26b], devoted
to the asymptotic stability problem for equivariant perturbations of the degree-one vortex in the
p1`2q-dimensional abelian Yang–Mills–Higgs model at the critical (self–dual) coupling.
1.1.The abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs model.The abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs model onp1`2q-

Static finite energy configurations in fixed topological degree classes are expected to play a

vacuum statespϕ,Aq “ peiκ,0q,κP r0,2πq, have trivial topological degree; their asymptotic

The existence of such vortices was proved in [BC89; Plo81]. [Gus02; GS00] established that
vortices of degree˘1 are (orbitally) stable for all values of the coupling constantλą0, while
higher-degree vortices|n| ě2 are orbitally stable for 0ăλă1 and unstable forλą1. The
asymptotic stability problem for the degree-one vortex has remained open.
The main result of this three-paper series is the asymptotic stability of the degree-one vortex
under the abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs equations (1.1) in the self-dual caseλ“1 for equivariant
perturbations that are small in a weighted Sobolev norm, see Theorem 1.1 in Subsection 1.6
below for the precise statement of the result. Throughout the remainder of this paper we restrict
to the self-dual couplingλ“1.

1.3.Hamiltonian formulation.We recast the self-dual abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs equations
(1.3) in Hamiltonian form. This formulation will be used to derive the evolution equations for
equivariant perturbations of the degree-one vortex and will also be important in parts of the
nonlinear analysis later on. As in Maxwell theory, the temporal component of the connection acts
as a Lagrange multiplier enforcing Gauss’ law; see, for instance, [Dir64; Wal84], and also [Rub88]
for a related derivation in a different gauge.

stability result for equivariant perturbations of the degree-one vortex under the evolution of the
self-dual abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs system of equations.

Remark 1.2.The constantsΓ 0ąΓ 1ą0in the statement of Theorem 1.1 are the Fermi Golden
Rule constants defined in(9.68)in the proof of Proposition 9.6. The fact thatΓ 1ą0is verified
in [L¨ uh+26a, Proposition 4.3] using rigorous numerics.
The core of the proof of Theorem 1.1 is the long-time analysis of the coupled system consisting
of the nonlinear Klein–Gordon equations (1.30) for the radiation term and the nonlinear oscillator
equations for the internal mode components. In two space dimensions, the dispersive decay of
Klein–Gordon waves is weak enough that the quadratic terms in the equation for the radiation
term cannot be treated perturbatively. We therefore develop a version of the space-time resonance
method with potential in the half-line setting. A key point is that much of the normal form analysis
is carried out relative to the flat Klein–Gordon flow, while transference estimates derived from
the distorted Fourier theory allow us to pass back to the Klein–Gordon flow generated by the
linearized operatorM.
The decay of the internal mode components is caused by their resonant nonlinear coupling to
the continuous spectrum, a mechanism known as nonlinear radiation damping. This mechanism

===== PAGE 10 =====
10 J. L ¨UHRMANN, J. M. PALACIOS, F. PUSATERI, W. SCHLAG, AND S. SHAHSHAHANI
naturally produces the time scalet„ε ´2 and explains the two regimes appearing in the decay
estimates (1.36) and (1.37). In the course of the proof we obtain more refined information on
both the radiation term and the internal mode components; see the bootstrap assumptions in
Subsection 3.4.
We refer to Section 2 for a detailed overview of the main ideas entering the proof of Theorem 1.1.
1.7.References.It is customary to distinguish between gauged (or magnetic) vortex models, in
which the scalar field is coupled to an electromagnetic field, and non-gauged (or global) vortex
models; see the standard monographs [JT80; MS04]. In both settings, one may further distinguish
between the static theory and the study of the dynamics.
The static theory of magnetic vortices in the abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs model at the self-dual
coupling was largely developed in [JT80; Tau80a; Tau80b]. See also [BC89; Plo81] for the equi-
variant setting. In the self-dual case vortices are energy minimizers within their degree class.
Using the Bogomolny structure, [JT80; Tau80a; Tau80b] proved the existence and uniqueness of
vortices for any finite prescribed vortex set, that is, the zeros of the scalar fieldϕ. Low energy
dynamics as geodesic motion on the moduli space of self-dual vortices was predicted in [Man82].
See also [MS04]. This geodesic approximation was verified near the self-dual regime and on a
large time interval in [Stu94]. Away from the critical coupling vortices are not necessary energy
minimizers in their energy class and may not be spectrally stable. For a detailed spectral analy-
sis and results on the dynamics of vortices away from the self-dual coupling see [Gus97; Gus02;
GS06]. For the hyperbolic abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs equations [BM85] proved global existence
in the Lorenz gauge for all values of the coupling constant. Asymptotic stability of the vacuum
statespϕ,Aq“pe iκ,0q,κPr0,2πq, was proved in [Tsu03]. For the related Schr¨ odinger flow, we
refer to [DS09], and for the corresponding heat flow dynamics to [DS97; Gus97; Gus02; Zha].
Related questions concerning vortex dynamics on Riemann surfaces have been studied in [Dem13;
LS25; Stu99].
The static solutions to the (non-gauged) complex Ginzburg-Landau equation in the plane are
also known as vortices, but they have infinite energy. Existence and uniqueness of equivariant
vortices were proved in [CEQ94; HH94]. Their dynamics have been studied extensively for the
associated nonlinear Schr¨ odinger equation, also known as the Gross-Pitaevskii equation. Global
existence for finite-energy data was established in [G´ er08], while the final-state problem for small
perturbations of the vacuum was considered in [GNT07]. Orbital stability of the degree-one
vortex was proved in [GPS22]; see also [PFK04; Wei91]. Further results on non-gauged vortex
dynamics can be found in [Neu90; OS97; OS98a; OS98b; OS02; PJM]. Detailed analyses of the
corresponding linearized operator, including the construction of the associated distorted Fourier
transform, were carried out in [CGP; LSS; PPa].
From a broader perspective, the present work is concerned with the dynamics of topological
solitons and more specifically with asymptotic stability problems in settings where relatively
weak dispersion and low-power nonlinearities interact with additional spectral obstructions, such
as internal modes or threshold resonances. The literature on asymptotic stability of solitons
is vast, and we do not attempt a comprehensive review here. General overviews and further
references can be found in the survey articles [CM21; Ger; KMM17b; Mar; Tao09]. For a sample
of related results in dimension one on the asymptotic stability of kinks in scalar field theories on
the line and of ground states of focusing nonlinear Klein-Gordon equations on the line we refer to
[AMP23; BP95; CLL; CL; CM23; Cuc+24; DM22; GP22; GPZ23; KP23; KY26; KK11; KM24;
KMM17a; KMM22; KNS12; KS06; LL23; LS23; LS24; PPb] and the references therein. For a
sample of related results in higher dimensions see [BKT13; BPT; BT14; GKT07; GKT08; KLS14;
KS07; Law+23; LOS17; Li22; LMZ18; NS11; NS12; Sch09] and the references therein. Important
progress has also been made on the stability of stationary solutions to quasilinear wave equations,
particularly in general relativity. Although a review of this literature lies beyond the scope of the

===== PAGE 11 =====
ASYMPTOTIC STABILITY OF THE DEGREE-ONE VORTEX IN THE ABELIAN YMH MODEL 11
present paper, we mention [Don+16], which is especially relevant to the discussion in Section 2,
and refer to [Oh] for a survey of some of the developments in the quasilinear setting.
A central aspect of this work is the decay, under the full nonlinear evolution, of the oscillations
associated with the internal mode. The nonlinear mechanism responsible for this decay is known
as radiation damping. The use of radiation damping to establish the decay of internal modes goes
back to [BP95; SW99]; see also [Sig93]. Since these pioneering works, the mechanism has been
established in a variety of models possessing internal modes; see, for example, [BC11; BS03; BP95;
CM23; CM08; GS07; GW08; KM24; KMM17a; LP; SW99; TY02] and the references therein, as
well as the survey article [CM21].
1.8.Organization of the paper.Section 2 provides an overview of the proof of Theorem 1.1.
We first explain the main difficulties and the mechanisms used to overcome them through a scalar
model problem, and then describe how these ideas are implemented for the full system.
In Section 3 we introduce the good-bad decomposition, which separates the spatially local-
ized interactions involving the internal modes from the purely radiative interactions, and the
flat-sharp decomposition, which isolates the component amenable to analysis using the standard
Fourier transform. We also introduce the corresponding decomposition of the temporal compo-
nent, classify the nonlinearities in Corollary 3.1, and formulate the bootstrap assumptions used
throughout the proof.
Section 4 collects the linear inputs established in our companion works. Proposition 4.1 records
the spectral properties of the linearized operatorMand the associated distorted Fourier trans-
form. We then state the dispersive and integrated local energy decay estimates for the flat and the
perturbed Klein-Gordon evolutions (see in particular Propositions 4.6 and 4.7). Finally, Propo-
sition 4.10 relates weighted norms of profiles defined using the flat Fourier transform to solutions
of the perturbed Klein–Gordon equation associated withM.
In Section 5 we estimate the temporal component. Proposition 5.1 establishes pointwise decay
and high Sobolev bounds for its good component, while Proposition 5.2 proves spatially weighted
pointwise and high Sobolev estimates for its bad component. In particular, the latter estimates
quantify the spatial localization inherited from the internal modes.
Section 6 contains the main nonlinear analysis for the auxiliary flat components of the good
radiation. We perform quadratic normal form transformations for the non-localized interactions
and incorporate the resulting terminal boundary corrections into the final data. Proposition 6.1
proves the weighted estimate for the corresponding renormalized profiles, while Proposition 6.2
gives their improved dispersive decay. Uniform high Sobolev and integrated local energy estimates
are proved in Propositions 6.3 and 6.4. At the end of the section, these auxiliary estimates onR 2
are transferred to the first angular modes defining the flat component of the good radiation.
In Section 7 we use the localization of the potential, together with the linear decay and trans-
ference estimates, to prove dispersive, high Sobolev, integrated local energy, and weighted profile
bounds for the sharp component (see Propositions 7.1, 7.2, and 7.3). Section 8 establishes the
corresponding bounds for the bad component (see Propositions 8.1, 8.2, and 8.3). The spatial
localization of its internal mode forcing yields the required pointwise decay, while the same forcing
produces the characteristict 1{2 growth of its weighted profile norm.
The internal mode analysis is carried out in Sections 9 and 10. In Section 9 we derive the
effective system of ODEs for the two internal mode profiles and use the constrained Hamiltonian
structure to identify the dissipative form generated by the Fermi Golden Rule. Proposition 9.6
reduces the decay law for|Z 1|2`|Z 2|2 to suitable estimates for the remainders in this system.
These estimates are established in Section 10 (see Proposition 10.1). The effective ODE analysis
in Section 9, combined with the remainder estimates in Section 10, establishes the nonlinear
radiation damping of the internal modes.

===== PAGE 12 =====
12 J. L ¨UHRMANN, J. M. PALACIOS, F. PUSATERI, W. SCHLAG, AND S. SHAHSHAHANI
Finally, in Section 11 we combine the estimates for the temporal component, the flat, sharp, and
bad radiation components, and the internal modes. We improve all the bootstrap assumptions,
close the continuation argument, and complete the proof of Theorem 1.1.

2.Overview of the Proof of Theorem 1.1
In this section we give an overview of the proof of Theorem 1.1, emphasizing the main difficulties
and the mechanisms used to overcome them. In Subsection 2.1 we organize the discussion around a
scalar model problem that captures many of the essential features of the full problem, including the
coupling between radiation and the internal mode, non-spatially localized quadratic interactions,
and derivative nonlinearities. In Subsection 2.2 we then explain how these ideas are implemented
for the full nonlinear system and discuss several additional aspects of the proof of Theorem 1.1.

We emphasize the modular nature of the flat-sharp approach developed here for proving decay.
It consists of two main components:
(i) a robust nonlinear analysis for the flat Klein-Gordon equation,
(ii) use of ILED estimates and transference relations for the passage between the flat analysis
and the Klein-Gordon evolution with potential.
Related instances of this general paradigm can be found, for example, in [DR10; RS04; Tat08].
In principle, each of the two components can be implemented by a variety of methods. For
the flat nonlinear analysis, we find the space-time resonance method particularly effective, as it
provides a streamlined framework for implementing normal forms for quadratic nonlinearities.
To establish the linear theory, especially ILED estimates and transference relations, we use the
distorted Fourier transform.
For the implementation of the flat-sharp approach we also find it instructive to draw analogies
with the classical vector field method. In Subsection 2.1.7 we informally describe how in that
context the flat-sharp decomposition would be implemented for a simpler cubic nonlinearity. The
interested reader may find this independent account clarifying before reading the discussion in
Subsections 2.1.3-2.1.6. Subsection 2.1.7 can also be safely skipped by the reader who is eager to
see the immediate implementation for (2.1) and the full problem, where we work entirely on the
Fourier side.

The non-negativity of Γ is evident from the explicit formula (2.12). Remarkably, it is by
now understood that this general structure is present for Hamiltonian equations, see for instance
[BC11]. Our full system (1.30) does not exactly fit into the framework of [BC11], or other related
existing works, because it is a system, rather than a scalar equation, and more importantly it is
not of Hamiltonian form in the classical sense. Instead, in view of the gauge freedom, to write
the system in Hamiltonian form one has to use the formalism of constrained Hamiltonians as in
Subsection 1.3. Nevertheless, we show that this structure is enough to derive a condition that is
analogous to the non-negativity of Γ in (2.11). For our explicit nonlinearity, we have chosen to
demonstrate this by direct computation rather than an abstract derivation, see Section 9.

2.1.5.Variable-coefficient quadratic normal forms.We now describe the main nonlinear analysis
for the flat component satisfying (2.16). Our treatment of the quadratic terms is based on a
variable-coefficient normal-form method. We recall that the use of the (classical) normal form
method for flat Klein-Gordon equations with constant-coefficient quadratic nonlinearities goes
back to [Sha85]. For the more recent development of variable-coefficient normal forms we refer
to [DM22; GP22; LLS21] and references therein. In particular, in this work we draw on ideas
from [GP22] for the implementation of variable coefficient normal forms with a principal value
singularity.
The two non-localized quadratic terms retained explicitly in the scalar model (2.16) present
different difficulties. Upon taking the Fourier transform, the vortex coefficient breaks the convo-
lution structure, and its Fourier transform introduces an additional frequency multiplier and a
principal value singularity. By contrast, the derivative quadratic nonlinearity retains the constant-
coefficient quadratic Klein-Gordon phase, which never vanishes, but its multiplier carries one input
derivative. In this overview we disregard the treatment of this latter as it is more standard.
Letfandg 5 denote the profiles ofuandv 5, as in (2.3) and (2.18). We also use the notation
f` :“fandf ´ :“f. For the contribution of the vortex Φ reu2 tog 5, a representative integral is

pΦrepσq“´iφ ď1pσqp.v. σ1

We note that atσ“0, the phase in (2.30) becomes the usual quadratic Klein-Gordon phase
´xξy`κ 1xηy`κ 2xξ´ηy,
which does not vanish. In a sufficiently small neighborhood ofσ“0, the phase then has a
positive lower bound for bounded frequencies. We can therefore divide by the phase and integrate
by parts in time in a suitable region containing the principal-value singularity. Outside this
suitably chosen neighborhood ofσ“0, the phase may vanish, giving rise to time resonances. But
there the principal value multiplier is regular in the sense that it furnishes spatial localization,
which permits the use of ILED-type estimates.

2.2.2.Spectral and linear input from the companion works [L¨ uh+26a; L¨ uh+26b].Our companion
papers [L¨ uh+26a; L¨ uh+26b] establish the spectral results and linear estimates that underpin the
nonlinear analysis in the present work. In this subsection, we review the aspects of those results
that are most relevant here.
In our first companion paper [L¨ uh+26a] we exploit the Bogomolny structure
L“B ˚B,(2.40)
with diagonal super-symmetric partner operator
L:“BB ˚“
ˆ
L1 0
0L 2
˙
,(2.41)
whereL 1 andL 2 are scalar, singular, radial Schr¨ odinger operators; see [L¨ uh+26a, Subsection 3.1].
Recall the block-diagonal structure of the linearized operatorMin terms ofLfrom (1.24). This
allows us to reduce the spectral analysis for the matrix operatorLto that of the scalar operators
L1 andL 2. Using this reduction, together with analytic arguments and rigorous numerics, in
[L¨ uh+26a] we prove that the continuous spectrum ofMis purely absolutely continuous and
equalsr1,8q, that the threshold 1 is neither an eigenvalue nor a resonance, and thatMhas a
unique gap eigenvalueλ 2Pp0,1qof multiplicity two. Its eigenspace gives the two internal modes,
and 2λą1, so their second harmonic lies in the continuous spectrum. More precisely,
λ2Pr0.777471875,0.777473750s.
In the same work we give a computer-assisted proof, with rigorous error analysis, of the fact that
the relevant Fermi Golden Rule non-degeneracy holds. These spectral conclusions are summarized
in Proposition 4.1.
We now briefly describe the numerical part of [L¨ uh+26a]. The calculation begins with quan-
titative estimates for the vortex profilepU,aq. Nearr“0, the vortex and the spectral solutions
are represented by Frobenius series, with explicit bounds for the terms left out of the finite sums.
For larger, comparison arguments and Volterra equations give bounds for the vortex and for the
solutions determined by their prescribed behavior at infinity. On the bounded interval between

===== PAGE 24 =====
24 J. L ¨UHRMANN, J. M. PALACIOS, F. PUSATERI, W. SCHLAG, AND S. SHAHSHAHANI
these two regions, interval arithmetic produces intervals containing the exact solutions and their
derivatives. The solutions constructed from the two ends are then compared at a common radius.
ForL 2, a threshold Wronskian bounded away from zero excludes a resonance, while opposite
signs of the eigenvalue Wronskian at the endpoints of the interval displayed above prove the ex-
istence of an eigenvalue inside that interval. An eigenvalue-counting estimate, whose integral is
also bounded rigorously, rules out a second eigenvalue. The corresponding assertions forL 1 are
proved analytically with a numerically assisted check that a potentialV 1ą1.
The verification of the Fermi Golden Rule reduces to evaluating explicit radial integrals involv-
ing the internal mode, the vortex, and the scalar generalized eigenfunctions. The contributions
near the origin are estimated from the Frobenius series, the integrals over the bounded region
are treated by interval arithmetic, and the contributions at infinity are controlled by the decay
estimates and the Volterra equations. This proves
D1ă0, D 2ă0, D 12ă0,(2.42)
for the coefficients appearing in (2.43) below. These inequalities are the non-degeneracy input
used to obtain the dissipative terms in the equations for the internal modes.
In our second companion paper [L¨ uh+26b] we develop the distorted Fourier theory forM, which
provides a spectral representation of the perturbed Klein-Gordon evolution on the continuous
spectral subspace. The reduction (2.40)-(2.41) relates this construction to the distorted Fourier
theories ofL 1 andL 2. As a consequence, we establish dispersive, integrated local energy, mapping,
and transference estimates for the matrix operatorMthat are summarized in Subsections 4.2–
4.4, see in particular Proposition 4.10. Note that the final results that are used in this article are
stated without reference to the distorted Fourier transform. Here we only point out the critical
inverse square decay of the potential inMthat makes the development of the distorted Fourier
theory in [L¨ uh+26b] especially challenging.

3.Setting up the Analysis
In this section we prepare the system of evolution equations (1.30), (1.31) for the radiation
termuptq, the temporal componentη 0ptq, and the internal mode componentsz 1ptq,z 2ptqfor the
nonlinear analysis in the remainder of this paper.
3.1.Good-bad decomposition.We will adopt a space-time resonances method approach to
establish decay for the radiation term. To this end we first decompose the radiation term as well
as the temporal component into a “good” part and a “bad” part, following [LP]. The “bad” part is
driven solely by nonlinear terms containing at least one internal mode component, which are thus
spatially localized, whereas the “good” part receives feedback only from nonlinear terms involving
radiation term components. As a consequence, we will see that the “good” part is amenable to
more favorable weighted energy estimates, which motivates the terminology. Instead, the weighted
energy bounds for the “bad” part are strongly growing. Nevertheless, we can obtain acceptable
dispersive decay estimates for the “bad” part by exploiting the spatial localization of its nonlinear
terms.
We introduce the notation

3.2.Flat-sharp decomposition.
3.2.1.Motivation.The analysis of the good partvptqof the radiation term is based on the space-
time resonances method. The main difficulty lies in the normal form analysis of the non-localized
quadratic interactions appearing in the nonlinearityN c. While the distorted Fourier transform
associated withMis well suited for linear estimates, it is less convenient for the nonlinear analysis
in this paper, in particular for the normal form analysis. For that reason we isolate a component
ofvptqwhose evolution is governed by the flat Klein-Gordon operator and can therefore be studied
using flat Fourier variables onR 2. The remaining contribution records the effect of the decaying
potential inM.
This leads to a second decomposition of the good partvptqof the radiation term, which we refer
to as itsflat-sharp decomposition. Roughly speaking, the flat component captures the asymptoti-
cally free Klein-Gordon dynamics responsible for the leading-order nonlinear interactions, whereas
the sharp component measures the discrepancy between the true evolution generated byMand
the flat model. Since this discrepancy is caused by a decaying potential, the forcing term for

===== PAGE 28 =====
28 J. L ¨UHRMANN, J. M. PALACIOS, F. PUSATERI, W. SCHLAG, AND S. SHAHSHAHANI
the sharp component is spatially localized. In order to recover acceptable bounds for the sharp
component, it is important that the flat component enjoys faster than linear decay. To this end
we introduce the flat component below as a solution to a suitably chosen final state problem.
To implement this idea, we first separate the free Klein-Gordon part of the linearized operator.
Recalling the definition ofM, we write

3.4.Bootstrap setup.By the local and global existence result from [BM85], the solution to the
system of evolution equations (1.30), (1.31) exists locally in time atH N
xpR2q-regularity on some
maximal interval of existencer0,T ˚qfor some 0ăT ˚ď8under the hypotheses of Theorem 1.1
and preserves the gauge condition (1.32). Moreover, if}pe iθuptq,eiθBtuptqq}HNx ˆHN´1
x
stays small
for all 0ďtăT ˚, then we haveT˚“8. We will recall in Section 11 how the results from [BM85]
apply in the context of the orthogonal gauge condition (1.19) in this work.
We establish global existence and the decay estimates (1.36) and (1.37) in part (i) of the
statement of Theorem 1.1 by a bootstrap argument. The additional lower bound (1.39) for the
internal mode in part (ii) does not require a separate bootstrap assumption. Once the bootstrap
estimates used for part (i) have been established, the lower bound in (1.39) follows directly from
part (ii) of Proposition 9.6 under the additional assumption (1.38).

Let us briefly indicate the role of the bootstrap norms. The bound (3.23) encodes the expected
Fermi Golden Rule decay of the internal mode components. The weighted Fourier bound (3.25) is
the main vector-field type norm used in the space-time resonance analysis of the flat component
of the good part of the radiation term. Its strong growth is caused by the quadratic internal mode
source terms in the evolution equation for the radiation term. The dispersive estimate (3.26) is
the pointwise decay bound needed in all nonlinear product estimates. The uniform-in-time high
Sobolev norm bound (3.27) allows us to absorb all the losses of derivatives that come up in our
arguments and helps to streamline some of the normal form analysis; for example, we can estimate
all the multi-linear symbols that appear in the analysis without too much effort, and do not need
to worry about the derivatives in the nonlinear terms. The integrated local energy decay estimate
at high Sobolev regularity (3.28) is primarily used in the derivation of the high Sobolev norm
bound (3.27).

4.Linear Theory
In this section we collect the linear theory used for the nonlinear analysis in this work. We first
recall the spectral properties of the linearized operatorMand the associated distorted Fourier
transform. We then record the linear decay estimates for the free Klein-Gordon evolution and
for the evolution generated byM. Next, we state a transference estimate that controls weighted
norms of flat profiles for solutions to inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equations associated withM
whose forcing terms are spatially localized. Finally, we record several auxiliary mapping properties
of the linearized operator. All proofs are contained in our companion works [L¨ uh+26a; L¨ uh+26b],
to which we give precise references below.

===== PAGE 36 =====
36 J. L ¨UHRMANN, J. M. PALACIOS, F. PUSATERI, W. SCHLAG, AND S. SHAHSHAHANI

description of the associated distorted Fourier transform are summarized in the next proposition.

orthogonal projection onto the continuous spectral subspace ofpL 2

5.Bounds for the Temporal Component
In this section we establish pointwise decay estimates and high Sobolev norm estimates for the
temporal componentη 0 and its time derivatives that will be used in the subsequent nonlinear
analysis. We treat separately the decomposition
η0“η 5
0,c`η7
0,c`η 0,d
introduced in Subsections 3.1 and 3.2. The flat componentη 5
0,c of the good part of the temporal
component is estimated directly from the flat elliptic equations (3.18) and (3.19). The sharp
componentη 7
0,c of the good part of the temporal component is then controlled in terms ofη 5
0,c
through the localized elliptic relation (3.17), while the bad partη 0,d of the temporal component is
estimated in spatially weighted norms by exploiting the spatial localization of the internal mode
components and the off-diagonal exponential decay of the Green’s function of´∆`U 2.

norms.

6.Bounds for the Flat Component of the Good Part
The purpose of this section is to derive weighted energy, dispersive, high Sobolev, and ILED
bounds for the auxiliary flat profilesg 5
reptqandg 5
imptqintroduced in (3.37). Recall that these
profiles are associated with the real-valued flat Klein–Gordon evolutionsv 5
reptqandv 5
imptq, which
solve (3.10) and (3.11) on the bootstrap intervalr0,Ts. The estimates obtained here are auxiliary
estimates outside symmetry. They are transferred in Subsection 6.6 to the first angular modes of
the flat component of the good part of the radiation term, which enter the decompositions of the
full flat profilesf reptqandf imptqin (3.35) and (3.36).
We remind the reader that the reason for imposing final data on the flat component is the sub-
sequent analysis of the sharp component. By (3.15), the sharp component is forced by´P cpVv5q,

===== PAGE 46 =====
46 J. L ¨UHRMANN, J. M. PALACIOS, F. PUSATERI, W. SCHLAG, AND S. SHAHSHAHANI
whereVis spatially localized. To close the estimates forv 7, it is important thatv 5 exhibit im-
proved decay coming directly from the nonlinear contributions rather than merely behave like a
generic linear Klein–Gordon wave. Our final-state formulation is designed to furnish this nonlinear
decay mechanism.
6.1.Summary of results and overview.We begin by summarizing the main results of this
section. The weighted energy estimate in the first proposition below is stated for the normal-
form-renormalized profile
g5
˚ptq´B ˚rf,fsptq,˚Ptre,imu,
rather than forg 5
˚ptqitself. The quadratic expressionB ˚rf,fsptqcollects all the boundary terms
generated by the normal form transformations of the non-spatially localized quadratic interactions.
Its precise definition is given in (6.3). The point of the final-state prescription (3.12)–(3.13) is
that the renormalized profile, not the raw profile, has zero final data at timeT.

The main difficulty in the derivation of the above bounds comes from the quadratic interactions
without spatial localization in the nonlinearities Re
`
eiθNc
˘
and Im
`
eiθNc
˘
of the auxiliary flat
Klein-Gordon equations (3.10) and (3.11). According to the classification in Corollary 3.1, the only
such contributions are those of types (1), (2), (5), and (6). These interactions cannot be estimated
directly in the profile equations and must instead be treated by normal form transformations,
i.e., by integrating by parts in time in the corresponding Duhamel integrals. The normal form
transformations convert the non-spatially localized quadratic interactions into effectively cubic or
at least spatially localized quadratic terms, but they also generate quadratic boundary terms at
the two endpoints of the intervalrt,Ts. This endpoint structure explains the choice of the final
data in (3.12)–(3.13). Ideally, one would like to impose zero final data for the flat component at
timet“T. However, if one imposed the raw conditiong 5
˚pTq“0,˚Ptre,imu, then the normal
form identity would leave a terminal quadratic boundary contributionB˚rf,fspTq. The available
weighted energy bounds for these quadratic boundary expressions are not compatible with the
desired improved dispersive decay estimate for the evolution of the renormalized profile. Instead,
we incorporate the terminal quadratic boundary term into the final state data. Equivalently,
the quantity which has zero final data at timeTis not the raw profileg 5
˚, but the normal-form-
renormalized profile
g5
˚ptq´B ˚rf,fsptq,˚Ptre,imu.
We now spell this out for the profileg 5
reptq. The other profileg 5
imptqcan be treated exactly in
the same way, and we omit the details. For times 0ďtďT, we obtain from (3.39) that

section. In Subsection 6.4 we establish the proofs of Propositions 6.1, 6.3, and 6.4 by systemat-
ically estimating, type by type, the contributions listed in Corollary 3.1. The dispersive bound
of Proposition 6.2 is then derived in Subsection 6.5, and the transfer estimates are stated in
Subsection 6.6.

Remark 6.11.The derivative bounds for the phase function established in Lemma 6.10 are far
from optimal; compare, for instance, with the sharper estimates in [HN16, Lemma 2.1]. Neverthe-
less, the bounds obtained here are entirely sufficient for the multilinear H¨ older-type estimates used
throughout the nonlinear analysis of this section. Indeed, our bootstrap framework propagates a
uniform-in-time bound on a sufficiently high Sobolev norm, allowing any low-frequency derivative
losses arising from Lemma 6.10 to be absorbed without difficulty.

The first line on the right-hand side of (6.48) when inserted back into (6.47) produces trilinear
terms, for which the corresponding weighted energy estimates can be obtained similarly as before
using the commutation identity (6.42). A minor point worth commenting on is that the derivative
in the nonlinearities such asu 1,re∇u3,re in the first line on the right-hand side of (6.48) gets
compensated by the outputp´∆`1q ´1∇apart from the case of high-high interactions. But for
high-high interactions derivatives can be transferred between the two inputs so that there is no
danger of a pile-up of too many derivatives on an input onto which a frequency derivative falls.
The quadratic nonlinearities in the second line on the right-hand side of (6.48) are spatially
localized, thus their contributions when inserted back into (6.47) can be easily estimated using
the dual ILED estimate from Lemma 4.4
The nonlinearities in the remaining lines on the right-hand side of (6.48) are at least of cubic
order, and their contributions are thus easier to bound. We omit the lengthy details for these
harmless contributions.
The contributions of terms of the type (6)–(10) fromQ nl
repsqin (6.39) when inserted back into
the second main term on the right-hand side of (6.37) are similar or easier to the previously
discussed cases. We leave the details to the reader.

The weighted energy estimates for the contribution of pQp2q
k,lpt,ξqare now obtained by an analogous
normal-form strategy as in the treatment of the singular contributionpQp1q
k,l,singpt,ξqin the preceding
Subsection 6.4.1.1 concerning contributions of type (1). The only new issue is the derivative on
one input: after dyadic localization, some fixed-output estimates are not directly summable over
the output frequency. In those cases we retain Littlewood–Paley orthogonality and square-sum
the output pieces. After reinserting the evolution equations for the profiles, the normal-form
remainders in particular give rise to non-localized cubic terms. For their weighted energy estimates
we use the commutation identity (6.42) withη“0. The high Sobolev norm estimate and the ILED
estimate for the contribution of pQp2q
k,lpt,ξqare proved in an analogous manner as the corresponding
bounds for the singular part pQp1q,γ
k,l,singpt,ξqin the preceding Subsections 6.4.1.2 and 6.4.1.3, up to
the additional dyadic square summation via Littlewood-Paley trichotomies. In what follows we
illustrate this mechanism at the example of the derivation of the weighted energy estimates for the
contribution of pQp2q
k,lpt,ξq, while we leave the analogous details for the high Sobolev norm estimate
and for the ILED estimate to the reader.

(6.69)
We now discuss schematically the weighted energy estimate, the high Sobolev norm estimate as
well as the ILED estimate for the terms on the right-hand side of (6.69). The first term on the
right-hand side is spatially localized, moreover Proposition 5.1 provides sufficiently fastL 8
x -decay
forB tη5
0,cpsq, and even provides decay for theH N
x -norm ofB tη5
0,cpsq. The weighted energy estimate
for the contribution of the first term can thus be closed easily using the dual ILED estimate from
Lemma 4.4. For the high Sobolev norm estimate we also use the dual ILED estimate from
Lemma 4.4, while for the ILED bound we use Lemma 4.5. The second term on the right-hand
side of (6.69) is essentially of the same type as contributions of type (2), which have already been
treated above. We point out that the derivative on one of the inputs gets compensated by the
inverse frequency weightxξy ´2ξon the output apart from the case of high-high interactions, but
in that case one can easily transfer derivatives between the two inputs. The third term on the
right-hand side of (6.69) is spatially localized and quadratic, whence essentially of the same type
as contributions of type (4), which have been discussed in detail above. Finally, the fourth term
on the right-hand side of (6.69) involves only terms of at least cubic order. We skip the details
for these tedious, but harmless terms.

(6.70)
We briefly discuss the weighted energy estimate, the high Sobolev norm estimate as well as the
ILED estimate for the terms on the right-hand side of (6.70). The first term on the right-hand
side of (6.70) can be treated exactly in the same manner as the first term on the right-hand side

contributions of type (1), which have already been treated above. The third term on the right-
hand side of (6.70) is again spatially localized and quadratic, whence essentially of the same type

the details for the tedious, but harmless higher-order nonlinearities in the fourth term on the
right-hand side of (6.70).

9.Internal Mode I: The System of ODEs and Fermi Golden Rule
In this section we first prove the constrained Hamiltonian formulation stated in Subsection
1.3, and then expand the perturbation Hamiltonian to obtain the almost Hamiltonian system of
Lemma 9.1. This structure will be used in the derivation of the effective equations for the internal
modes and in the computation of the Fermi Golden Rule coefficients. In Subsection 9.2 we write
Duhamel’s formula for the profile of the perturbation and organize the various nonlinear terms
according to their homogeneity and dependence on the continuous or discrete components. We
are then in a position to study the system of ODEs satisfied by the internal modes. In Subsection
9.3, exploiting the Hamiltonian and block structure of the system, we calculate the relevant Fermi
Golden Rule coefficients that provide dissipation for the internal modes’ amplitudes; the neces-
sary non-degeneracy assumption is guaranteed by the analysis and numerical work in [L¨ uh+26a].
Moreover, we also organize all the non-leading order dissipative terms in the ODE, and provide
sufficient conditions (see Lemma 9.5) so that these can be considered as remainders relative to the
dissipative dynamics. The estimates for all the remainder terms are then carried out in Section 10.
9.1.Hamiltonian structure.We begin by giving the details of the derivation of the constrained
Hamiltonian formulation stated in Subsection 1.3 and the validity of (1.16)-(1.17). We then
specialize the perturbation Hamiltonian to the equivariant variables and separate its quadratic,
cubic, quartic, and constraint contributions to arrive at the main equations (1.20)-(1.23)
Proof of(1.16)-(1.17).Recall the position and momentum variables introduced in (1.9)–(1.10).
Since the Lagrangian does not containB 0A0, the temporal componentA 0 has no conjugate mo-
mentum and is treated as an independent, non-dynamical variable. The velocities can be expressed
in terms of the momenta as
B0ϕ“´2p ϕ`iA 0qϕ,B 0Aj“B jA0´pAj, j“1,2.
After this substitution, the Lagrangian density becomes

9.3.Fermi Golden Rule.We now analyze the ODE for the profile of the internal mode. We
first write out the systems of ODE for the internal modesZ j in§9.3.1, and organize the various
nonlinear terms. Then, in§9.3.2 we prove that the ODE dynamics lead to damping of the
amplitudes of the internal modes provided certain coefficients are proven to be non-zero, and that
we have suitable estimates on sub-leading order terms. The verification of the necessary non-
vanishing conditions (9.50) is guaranteed by [L¨ uh+26a, Proposition 4.3]. The estimates needed
on all remainder terms are proven in Section 10.

11.Conclusion of the Proof of Theorem 1.1
In this final section we detail how the results from the preceding sections combine to furnish a
proof of Theorem 1.1.
Proof of Theorem 1.1.We begin by explaining how the local and global existence result for the
abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs equation in the Lorenz gauge from [BM85] applies in the present gauge
to furnish a local-in-time solution to (1.3) atH N
x -regularity on some maximal interval of existence
r0,T˚q. [BM85] establishes that for initial data
`
η0,Btη0,ηx1,Btηx2,ηx2,Btηx2,φ,D 0φ
˘ˇˇ
t“0PH psq :“
`
Hs`1
x pR2qˆH s
xpR2q
˘4, sě2,
satisfying the Lorenz gauge condition∇µηµ“0, the abelian Yang-Mills-Higgs system of equations
(1.3) withϕ“Φ `φandA“a`η, admits a unique solution defined globally in time. Note
that in the Lorenz gaugeA µ,µ“0,1,2,3, andϕall satisfy wave-type equations (see for instance
[BM85, equation (2.19)]), and the initial data are subject to the constraint ∆A 0 “Imp ϕD0ϕq
(which in our case is just the elliptic equation (1.21) forη 0; see also [BM85, equation (2.11)]).
Now given sufficiently regular data in a different gauge, such as Stuart’s gauge (1.18) in our case,
one can obtain data satisfying the Lorenz gauge condition by means of a gauge transformation.
Indeed, suppose we are given initial data satisfying Stuart’s gauge condition (1.18). These data
determineA 0 by means of the elliptic equation (1.21). Then the modified initial data
˚ϕp0q“ϕp0q, ˚Aµp0q“A µp0q,
Bt˚ϕp0q“B tϕp0q,B t ˚Ajp0q“B tAjp0q,B t ˚A0p0q“∇ jAjp0q,
satisfy the Lorenz gauge condition∇ µ ˚Aµ “0 as well as the constraint (1.21) at timet“0.
It follows from [BM85] that there is a unique global evolution of this data satisfying equations
[BM85, equation (2.19)] in Lorenz gauge 5 and the constraint [BM85, equation (2.11)]. Moreover,
the resulting solution is equivariant if the original (and hence the modified) data are equivariant.
To return to a solution in Stuart’s gauge, we decompose ˚ϕas ˚ϕ“Φ `ψ. Ifχsatisfies the elliptic
equation (see [Stu94, p.73])
´∆χ`|Φ |2 sinχ`Im

short time, observe that ifφandηare small, say inH N
xpR2q, then∇ j ˚Aj andψremain small in

xpR2qfor small time. Existence of a solution to (11.1) then follows from standard perturbative
methods. We also need to verify that the initial data forpe iχ˚ϕ, ˚A`dχqagree with our original
data. This follows ifχp0q“B tχp0q“0, which in turn follows from evaluating (11.1) and its time
derivative at timet“0, and comparing the initial constraints (1.21) and [BM85, equation (2.11)].
Having thus obtained a local-in-time solution6, we can start our bootstrap (3.23)–(3.28) on some
intervalr0,Ts. Once these bootstrap assumptions have been improved, the resulting uniform
bounds allow us to continue the solution and obtain global existence. It therefore remains to
explain how the estimates established in the preceding sections improve (3.23) and (3.25)–(3.28).
This closes the bootstrap and proves part (i) of Theorem 1.1. Under the additional assumption
(1.38) of part (ii), no further bootstrap argument is needed: the lower bound in (1.39) will follow
directly from part (ii) of Proposition 9.6. We also note that the pointwise decay estimate (1.37)
5Incidentally, the modified data still satisfy Stuart’s gauge condition att“0 as well as (1.21). But since the

for the radiation follows from the improved dispersive bound (3.26), while for very short times it
follows by Sobolev embedding from the high Sobolev bound (3.27).
First, the bootstrap bound (3.23) about the decay estimate for the internal mode components
is improved as a consequence of Propositions 9.6 and 10.1. More precisely, under our bootstrap
assumptions, Proposition 10.1 implies that the hypotheses (9.51), (9.52), (9.53), (9.54) of Propo-
sition 9.6 are satisfied. The latter then implies that the bootstrap bound (3.23) can be improved.
Moreover, part (ii) of Proposition 9.6 shows that, under the additional assumption (1.38) in part
(ii) of the statement of Theorem 1.1, the lower bound in (1.39) for the decay of the internal mode
components follows directly.
